========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly, what finally pushed me to come in is this constant tightness in my body. My shoulders and neck are just… clenched all day. It feels like I’m bracing myself for a classroom observation that never ends, if that makes sense. I teach preschool, so I’m used to being “on,” but this is different—my body just won’t let go.

By the time I get home in the evening, my jaw aches from being so tense, and it’s gotten to the point where I can hardly enjoy playing guitar, which is one of the few things I usually really look forward to. I’ll pick it up and realize I’m still holding my neck and face so tight that it’s not relaxing at all. It’s just wearing me down, and I thought… okay, I probably need to talk to someone about this.

Yes—definitely. It’s not just tightness like, “oh, I’m a little stiff.” My shoulders and neck actually hurt from being clenched so much. It feels like they’re locked up all day, like I’m holding myself in this braced position and can’t quite release it.

Yes. Sleep has been… pretty bad, honestly. Most nights I get into bed already exhausted, but then my mind just starts running. I’ll lie there for hours replaying things—like interview answers, or whether I said something wrong, or bills I need to pay, or licensing forms I haven’t dealt with yet. Yes, very much. That’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because I’m not just sitting there avoiding things—I’ll be trying to do them, and my brain just won’t stay on the page.

My eyes are moving across the words, but my attention has already jumped somewhere else—like what an administrator might think of what I wrote, or whether I missed some crucial detail that’s going to come back and embarrass me later.

And then I’ll go back to check it, but checking it doesn’t really reassure me. It just makes the whole thing take twice as long. Something that should be straightforward ends up feeling like this huge task because I keep losing the thread. At work especially, lesson planning used to feel pretty natural to me, and now I can get stuck on one small part because I’m second-guessing every word or every choice.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… although it’s less like I’m avoiding family dinners or something, and more that I’ve pulled back from the school-related things that used to make me feel connected to people.

I used to volunteer for story-time pretty readily, for example. I liked doing that. Reading to the kids, picking out the books, making it feel fun—that used to be one of the parts of teaching that felt most natural to me. But lately I won’t put myself forward for it unless someone else is handling the planning side. If I have to choose the story, think through the timing, worry about whether it’s appropriate or engaging enough, I just get so wound up that I’d rather not do it at all.

And it’s also affected trying to get back into the classroom. I’ve missed application deadlines because I kept putting off finishing things or checking them over too many times. I’ve gone into interviews and just… stumbled through them. I’ll know what I want to say, but I get so caught up in whether I’m answering correctly that I lose my train of thought. So yes, I guess I am avoiding things, or at least stepping back from them, and it’s making it hard to get back into teaching in any reliable way.

It’s more like my shoulders and neck are just set in this clenched position from the time I’m at work onward, like I’m bracing myself for something—like a classroom observation that never actually ends.\n\nIt probably becomes harder to ignore as the day goes on. By evening, my jaw is aching too, like I’ve been holding my face tight without realizing it. That’s when it really hits me, because I’ll try to sit down and play guitar, which usually helps me unwind, and I can’t really enjoy it. I’m still so tense in my neck and jaw that even doing something I like feels uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel pain in your shoulders, neck, or jaw when you're tense?\n\nPatient: Yes—definitely. It’s not just tightness like, “oh, I’m a little stiff.” My shoulders and neck actually hurt from being clenched so much. It feels like they’re locked up all day, like I’m holding myself in this braced position and can’t quite release it.\n\nAnd then by the evening it’s my jaw too. It starts aching pretty badly, like I’ve been clenching my teeth or holding my face tense without noticing. That’s honestly one of the things that made me think I needed to come in, because I’ll try to do something I usually enjoy, like playing guitar, and I can hardly relax into it. I’m sitting there with my neck and jaw hurting, and it kind of ruins the one thing that’s supposed to help me unwind.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sleep has been… pretty bad, honestly. Most nights I get into bed already exhausted, but then my mind just starts running. I’ll lie there for hours replaying things—like interview answers, or whether I said something wrong, or bills I need to pay, or licensing forms I haven’t dealt with yet. It’s like once the house is quiet, my brain decides that’s the perfect time to review every possible problem.\n\nAnd even when I do finally fall asleep, it doesn’t feel like I’m getting real rest. I’ll wake up around four in the morning, already tense, already sort of calculating what could go wrong that day. Before I’ve even gotten out of bed, I’m thinking through everything I need to handle and all the ways it could fall apart. So yes, sleep has definitely been a problem.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble concentrating or completing tasks at work or at home because of how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. That’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because I’m not just sitting there avoiding things—I’ll be trying to do them, and my brain just won’t stay on the page.\n\nLike if I’m filling out applications, or even trying to plan what should be a simple lesson for the kids, I’ll read the same paragraph over and over and realize I haven’t taken it in at all. My eyes are moving across the words, but my attention has already jumped somewhere else—like what an administrator might think of what I wrote, or whether I missed some crucial detail that’s going to come back and embarrass me later.\n\nAnd then I’ll go back to check it, but checking it doesn’t really reassure me. It just makes the whole thing take twice as long. Something that should be straightforward ends up feeling like this huge task because I keep losing the thread. At work especially, lesson planning used to feel pretty natural to me, and now I can get stuck on one small part because I’m second-guessing every word or every choice.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or activities with friends and family because of how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes… although it’s less like I’m avoiding family dinners or something, and more that I’ve pulled back from the school-related things that used to make me feel connected to people.\n\nI used to volunteer for story-time pretty readily, for example. I liked doing that. Reading to the kids, picking out the books, making it feel fun—that used to be one of the parts of teaching that felt most natural to me. But lately I won’t put myself forward for it unless someone else is handling the planning side. If I have to choose the story, think through the timing, worry about whether it’s appropriate or engaging enough, I just get so wound up that I’d rather not do it at all.\n\nAnd it’s also affected trying to get back into the classroom. I’ve missed application deadlines because I kept putting off finishing things or checking them over too many times. I’ve gone into interviews and just… stumbled through them. I’ll know what I want to say, but I get so caught up in whether I’m answering correctly that I lose my train of thought. So yes, I guess I am avoiding things, or at least stepping back from them, and it’s making it hard to get back into teaching in any reliable way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes. I mean, I’m not really functioning the way I need to be, professionally. I keep telling myself I’m still a teacher, that I still know how to do the work, but actually getting back into a classroom has become… much harder than it should be.

I’ve missed application deadlines because I couldn’t get the materials finished or I kept going back over them again and again until suddenly the deadline had passed. And when I do get to an interview, I stumble through it. I’ll know what I want to say, but I get so caught up in whether I’m saying it the “right” way that I lose the thread.

Even with something like story-time, which used to be simple and honestly one of the parts I enjoyed most, I don’t volunteer for it now unless someone else is handling the planning. If I have to choose the book and think through the timing and make all the little decisions, I start second-guessing everything.

So yes, it’s definitely interfering. It’s keeping me from getting back into the classroom in any reliable way.

It feels like they’re locked up all day, like I’m holding myself in this braced position and can’t quite release it.\n\nAnd then by the evening it’s my jaw too. It starts aching pretty badly, like I’ve been clenching my teeth or holding my face tense without noticing. That’s honestly one of the things that made me think I needed to come in, because I’ll try to do something I usually enjoy, like playing guitar, and I can hardly relax into it. I’m sitting there with my neck and jaw hurting, and it kind of ruins the one thing that’s supposed to help me unwind.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sleep has been… pretty bad, honestly. Most nights I get into bed already exhausted, but then my mind just starts running. I’ll lie there for hours replaying things—like interview answers, or whether I said something wrong, or bills I need to pay, or licensing forms I haven’t dealt with yet. It’s like once the house is quiet, my brain decides that’s the perfect time to review every possible problem.\n\nAnd even when I do finally fall asleep, it doesn’t feel like I’m getting real rest. I’ll wake up around four in the morning, already tense, already sort of calculating what could go wrong that day. Before I’ve even gotten out of bed, I’m thinking through everything I need to handle and all the ways it could fall apart. So yes, sleep has definitely been a problem.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble concentrating or completing tasks at work or at home because of how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. That’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because I’m not just sitting there avoiding things—I’ll be trying to do them, and my brain just won’t stay on the page.\n\nLike if I’m filling out applications, or even trying to plan what should be a simple lesson for the kids, I’ll read the same paragraph over and over and realize I haven’t taken it in at all. My eyes are moving across the words, but my attention has already jumped somewhere else—like what an administrator might think of what I wrote, or whether I missed some crucial detail that’s going to come back and embarrass me later.\n\nAnd then I’ll go back to check it, but checking it doesn’t really reassure me. It just makes the whole thing take twice as long. Something that should be straightforward ends up feeling like this huge task because I keep losing the thread. At work especially, lesson planning used to feel pretty natural to me, and now I can get stuck on one small part because I’m second-guessing every word or every choice.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or activities with friends and family because of how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes… although it’s less like I’m avoiding family dinners or something, and more that I’ve pulled back from the school-related things that used to make me feel connected to people.\n\nI used to volunteer for story-time pretty readily, for example. I liked doing that. Reading to the kids, picking out the books, making it feel fun—that used to be one of the parts of teaching that felt most natural to me. But lately I won’t put myself forward for it unless someone else is handling the planning side. If I have to choose the story, think through the timing, worry about whether it’s appropriate or engaging enough, I just get so wound up that I’d rather not do it at all.\n\nAnd it’s also affected trying to get back into the classroom. I’ve missed application deadlines because I kept putting off finishing things or checking them over too many times. I’ve gone into interviews and just… stumbled through them. I’ll know what I want to say, but I get so caught up in whether I’m answering correctly that I lose my train of thought. So yes, I guess I am avoiding things, or at least stepping back from them, and it’s making it hard to get back into teaching in any reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to fulfill your responsibilities as a preschool teacher due to how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I’m not really functioning the way I need to be, professionally. I keep telling myself I’m still a teacher, that I still know how to do the work, but actually getting back into a classroom has become… much harder than it should be.\n\nI’ve missed application deadlines because I couldn’t get the materials finished or I kept going back over them again and again until suddenly the deadline had passed. And when I do get to an interview, I stumble through it. I’ll know what I want to say, but I get so caught up in whether I’m saying it the “right” way that I lose the thread.\n\nEven with something like story-time, which used to be simple and honestly one of the parts I enjoyed most, I don’t volunteer for it now unless someone else is handling the planning. If I have to choose the book and think through the timing and make all the little decisions, I start second-guessing everything.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely interfering. It’s keeping me from getting back into the classroom in any reliable way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s just wearing me down, and I thought… okay, I probably need to talk to someone about this.\n\nDoctor: Is the tightness in your body constant throughout the day, or does it get worse at specific times or when you're doing certain activities?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty much there all day, honestly. I wouldn’t say it completely comes and goes. It’s more like my shoulders and neck are just set in this clenched position from the time I’m at work onward, like I’m bracing myself for something—like a classroom observation that never actually ends.\n\nIt probably becomes harder to ignore as the day goes on. By evening, my jaw is aching too, like I’ve been holding my face tight without realizing it. That’s when it really hits me, because I’ll try to sit down and play guitar, which usually helps me unwind, and I can’t really enjoy it. I’m still so tense in my neck and jaw that even doing something I like feels uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel pain in your shoulders, neck, or jaw when you're tense?\n\nPatient: Yes—definitely. It’s not just tightness like, “oh, I’m a little stiff.” My shoulders and neck actually hurt from being clenched so much. It feels like they’re locked up all day, like I’m holding myself in this braced position and can’t quite release it.\n\nAnd then by the evening it’s my jaw too. It starts aching pretty badly, like I’ve been clenching my teeth or holding my face tense without noticing. That’s honestly one of the things that made me think I needed to come in, because I’ll try to do something I usually enjoy, like playing guitar, and I can hardly relax into it. I’m sitting there with my neck and jaw hurting, and it kind of ruins the one thing that’s supposed to help me unwind.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sleep has been… pretty bad, honestly. Most nights I get into bed already exhausted, but then my mind just starts running. I’ll lie there for hours replaying things—like interview answers, or whether I said something wrong, or bills I need to pay, or licensing forms I haven’t dealt with yet. It’s like once the house is quiet, my brain decides that’s the perfect time to review every possible problem.\n\nAnd even when I do finally fall asleep, it doesn’t feel like I’m getting real rest. I’ll wake up around four in the morning, already tense, already sort of calculating what could go wrong that day. Before I’ve even gotten out of bed, I’m thinking through everything I need to handle and all the ways it could fall apart. So yes, sleep has definitely been a problem.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble concentrating or completing tasks at work or at home because of how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. That’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because I’m not just sitting there avoiding things—I’ll be trying to do them, and my brain just won’t stay on the page.\n\nLike if I’m filling out applications, or even trying to plan what should be a simple lesson for the kids, I’ll read the same paragraph over and over and realize I haven’t taken it in at all. My eyes are moving across the words, but my attention has already jumped somewhere else—like what an administrator might think of what I wrote, or whether I missed some crucial detail that’s going to come back and embarrass me later.\n\nAnd then I’ll go back to check it, but checking it doesn’t really reassure me. It just makes the whole thing take twice as long. Something that should be straightforward ends up feeling like this huge task because I keep losing the thread. At work especially, lesson planning used to feel pretty natural to me, and now I can get stuck on one small part because I’m second-guessing every word or every choice.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or activities with friends and family because of how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes… although it’s less like I’m avoiding family dinners or something, and more that I’ve pulled back from the school-related things that used to make me feel connected to people.\n\nI used to volunteer for story-time pretty readily, for example. I liked doing that. Reading to the kids, picking out the books, making it feel fun—that used to be one of the parts of teaching that felt most natural to me. But lately I won’t put myself forward for it unless someone else is handling the planning side. If I have to choose the story, think through the timing, worry about whether it’s appropriate or engaging enough, I just get so wound up that I’d rather not do it at all.\n\nAnd it’s also affected trying to get back into the classroom. I’ve missed application deadlines because I kept putting off finishing things or checking them over too many times. I’ve gone into interviews and just… stumbled through them. I’ll know what I want to say, but I get so caught up in whether I’m answering correctly that I lose my train of thought. So yes, I guess I am avoiding things, or at least stepping back from them, and it’s making it hard to get back into teaching in any reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to fulfill your responsibilities as a preschool teacher due to how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I’m not really functioning the way I need to be, professionally. I keep telling myself I’m still a teacher, that I still know how to do the work, but actually getting back into a classroom has become… much harder than it should be.\n\nI’ve missed application deadlines because I couldn’t get the materials finished or I kept going back over them again and again until suddenly the deadline had passed. And when I do get to an interview, I stumble through it. I’ll know what I want to say, but I get so caught up in whether I’m saying it the “right” way that I lose the thread.\n\nEven with something like story-time, which used to be simple and honestly one of the parts I enjoyed most, I don’t volunteer for it now unless someone else is handling the planning. If I have to choose the book and think through the timing and make all the little decisions, I start second-guessing everything.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely interfering. It’s keeping me from getting back into the classroom in any reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your relationships with family and friends are being affected by how you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Not in a way I can point to clearly, like, “this relationship has changed” or “we had a big conflict because of it.” I don’t think I’ve noticed anything that definite with family or friends.\n\nMost of what I’m aware of has been around work and school-related situations, so when you ask about relationships, I have to sort of stop and think. I suppose people close to me may notice I’m not quite as available or as easygoing, but I don’t know that I can say it’s really affected the relationships themselves. I haven’t been tracking it that way.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is, I’m not sure. Nothing obvious comes to mind.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Aside from guitar… the biggest thing is really the school-related stuff, the activities that used to feel like part of who I was as a teacher.

Story-time is the clearest example. I used to volunteer for that without really thinking twice. I liked choosing the books and figuring out how to make it engaging for the kids. But now I’ve pretty much stopped offering unless someone else is doing the planning part. If someone says, “We already picked the book, we just need you to read,” I can usually manage that. But if I have to decide what to read, how long it should take, whether it’s the right choice, whether I’ll keep their attention… I get so tangled up in it that I just don’t put myself forward.

And it’s not only that one activity. Trying to get back into the classroom at all has been affected. I’ve missed application deadlines because I keep putting off finishing things or checking them over and over, and then the deadline is gone. I’ve had interviews where I just stumble through, even though I know I’m qualified and I know what I mean to say. So yes, I’ve reduced things quite a bit. It feels like I’m stepping back from the parts of teaching that used to connect me to the work, and it’s keeping me from getting back into a classroom in any steady way.

Before I’ve even gotten out of bed, I’m thinking through everything I need to handle and all the ways it could fall apart. So yes, sleep has definitely been a problem.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble concentrating or completing tasks at work or at home because of how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. That’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because I’m not just sitting there avoiding things—I’ll be trying to do them, and my brain just won’t stay on the page.\n\nLike if I’m filling out applications, or even trying to plan what should be a simple lesson for the kids, I’ll read the same paragraph over and over and realize I haven’t taken it in at all. My eyes are moving across the words, but my attention has already jumped somewhere else—like what an administrator might think of what I wrote, or whether I missed some crucial detail that’s going to come back and embarrass me later.\n\nAnd then I’ll go back to check it, but checking it doesn’t really reassure me. It just makes the whole thing take twice as long. Something that should be straightforward ends up feeling like this huge task because I keep losing the thread. At work especially, lesson planning used to feel pretty natural to me, and now I can get stuck on one small part because I’m second-guessing every word or every choice.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or activities with friends and family because of how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes… although it’s less like I’m avoiding family dinners or something, and more that I’ve pulled back from the school-related things that used to make me feel connected to people.\n\nI used to volunteer for story-time pretty readily, for example. I liked doing that. Reading to the kids, picking out the books, making it feel fun—that used to be one of the parts of teaching that felt most natural to me. But lately I won’t put myself forward for it unless someone else is handling the planning side. If I have to choose the story, think through the timing, worry about whether it’s appropriate or engaging enough, I just get so wound up that I’d rather not do it at all.\n\nAnd it’s also affected trying to get back into the classroom. I’ve missed application deadlines because I kept putting off finishing things or checking them over too many times. I’ve gone into interviews and just… stumbled through them. I’ll know what I want to say, but I get so caught up in whether I’m answering correctly that I lose my train of thought. So yes, I guess I am avoiding things, or at least stepping back from them, and it’s making it hard to get back into teaching in any reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to fulfill your responsibilities as a preschool teacher due to how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I’m not really functioning the way I need to be, professionally. I keep telling myself I’m still a teacher, that I still know how to do the work, but actually getting back into a classroom has become… much harder than it should be.\n\nI’ve missed application deadlines because I couldn’t get the materials finished or I kept going back over them again and again until suddenly the deadline had passed. And when I do get to an interview, I stumble through it. I’ll know what I want to say, but I get so caught up in whether I’m saying it the “right” way that I lose the thread.\n\nEven with something like story-time, which used to be simple and honestly one of the parts I enjoyed most, I don’t volunteer for it now unless someone else is handling the planning. If I have to choose the book and think through the timing and make all the little decisions, I start second-guessing everything.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely interfering. It’s keeping me from getting back into the classroom in any reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your relationships with family and friends are being affected by how you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Not in a way I can point to clearly, like, “this relationship has changed” or “we had a big conflict because of it.” I don’t think I’ve noticed anything that definite with family or friends.\n\nMost of what I’m aware of has been around work and school-related situations, so when you ask about relationships, I have to sort of stop and think. I suppose people close to me may notice I’m not quite as available or as easygoing, but I don’t know that I can say it’s really affected the relationships themselves. I haven’t been tracking it that way.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is, I’m not sure. Nothing obvious comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or hobbies, aside from playing guitar, that you've had to stop or significantly reduce due to how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Aside from guitar… the biggest thing is really the school-related stuff, the activities that used to feel like part of who I was as a teacher.\n\nStory-time is the clearest example. I used to volunteer for that without really thinking twice. I liked choosing the books and figuring out how to make it engaging for the kids. But now I’ve pretty much stopped offering unless someone else is doing the planning part. If someone says, “We already picked the book, we just need you to read,” I can usually manage that. But if I have to decide what to read, how long it should take, whether it’s the right choice, whether I’ll keep their attention… I get so tangled up in it that I just don’t put myself forward.\n\nAnd it’s not only that one activity. Trying to get back into the classroom at all has been affected. I’ve missed application deadlines because I keep putting off finishing things or checking them over and over, and then the deadline is gone. I’ve had interviews where I just stumble through, even though I know I’m qualified and I know what I mean to say. So yes, I’ve reduced things quite a bit. It feels like I’m stepping back from the parts of teaching that used to connect me to the work, and it’s keeping me from getting back into a classroom in any steady way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your overall mood or emotional state since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that clearly. I mean, I know I don’t feel like myself in some ways, but if you’re asking whether I’ve had a major mood change, like feeling suddenly very low or unusually up or something like that, I don’t know that I can point to anything specific.\n\nIt’s more that my emotional state feels… crowded, if that makes sense. But I haven’t really been tracking it as a mood change. I’ve been so focused on whether I can get things done and whether I’m managing situations properly that I haven’t stepped back and thought, “My overall mood is different now.”\n\nSo I guess I’d say I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed one clear, significant change that I can describe neatly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think I am. Maybe more than I want to admit.

It’s not just “Will they like my lesson?” or “Will the kids stay engaged?” It turns into this whole chain in my head. I start wondering whether anyone will hire me again, whether I’ve somehow damaged my reputation with parents or other teachers, whether people already see me as unreliable or not really fit for the classroom anymore. And then it keeps going — like, if I make a small mistake during story-time, choose the wrong book, lose the children’s attention for a minute, stumble over something… my mind jumps to, “Does that mean I shouldn’t be trusted with children?” Which sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but that’s where my thinking goes.

And then there’s the bigger picture too. I’ll start worrying about whether I’ve messed up my whole future, whether retirement is getting farther away because I’m not getting back into a steady position, whether I’ve let too much time pass. So yes, I do feel afraid of being evaluated. It’s like every little teaching-related moment feels like it could confirm the worst thing I’m already afraid people might think about me.

I’m sitting there with my neck and jaw hurting, and it kind of ruins the one thing that’s supposed to help me unwind.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sleep has been… pretty bad, honestly. Most nights I get into bed already exhausted, but then my mind just starts running. I’ll lie there for hours replaying things—like interview answers, or whether I said something wrong, or bills I need to pay, or licensing forms I haven’t dealt with yet. It’s like once the house is quiet, my brain decides that’s the perfect time to review every possible problem.\n\nAnd even when I do finally fall asleep, it doesn’t feel like I’m getting real rest. I’ll wake up around four in the morning, already tense, already sort of calculating what could go wrong that day. Before I’ve even gotten out of bed, I’m thinking through everything I need to handle and all the ways it could fall apart. So yes, sleep has definitely been a problem.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble concentrating or completing tasks at work or at home because of how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. That’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because I’m not just sitting there avoiding things—I’ll be trying to do them, and my brain just won’t stay on the page.\n\nLike if I’m filling out applications, or even trying to plan what should be a simple lesson for the kids, I’ll read the same paragraph over and over and realize I haven’t taken it in at all. My eyes are moving across the words, but my attention has already jumped somewhere else—like what an administrator might think of what I wrote, or whether I missed some crucial detail that’s going to come back and embarrass me later.\n\nAnd then I’ll go back to check it, but checking it doesn’t really reassure me. It just makes the whole thing take twice as long. Something that should be straightforward ends up feeling like this huge task because I keep losing the thread. At work especially, lesson planning used to feel pretty natural to me, and now I can get stuck on one small part because I’m second-guessing every word or every choice.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or activities with friends and family because of how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes… although it’s less like I’m avoiding family dinners or something, and more that I’ve pulled back from the school-related things that used to make me feel connected to people.\n\nI used to volunteer for story-time pretty readily, for example. I liked doing that. Reading to the kids, picking out the books, making it feel fun—that used to be one of the parts of teaching that felt most natural to me. But lately I won’t put myself forward for it unless someone else is handling the planning side. If I have to choose the story, think through the timing, worry about whether it’s appropriate or engaging enough, I just get so wound up that I’d rather not do it at all.\n\nAnd it’s also affected trying to get back into the classroom. I’ve missed application deadlines because I kept putting off finishing things or checking them over too many times. I’ve gone into interviews and just… stumbled through them. I’ll know what I want to say, but I get so caught up in whether I’m answering correctly that I lose my train of thought. So yes, I guess I am avoiding things, or at least stepping back from them, and it’s making it hard to get back into teaching in any reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to fulfill your responsibilities as a preschool teacher due to how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I’m not really functioning the way I need to be, professionally. I keep telling myself I’m still a teacher, that I still know how to do the work, but actually getting back into a classroom has become… much harder than it should be.\n\nI’ve missed application deadlines because I couldn’t get the materials finished or I kept going back over them again and again until suddenly the deadline had passed. And when I do get to an interview, I stumble through it. I’ll know what I want to say, but I get so caught up in whether I’m saying it the “right” way that I lose the thread.\n\nEven with something like story-time, which used to be simple and honestly one of the parts I enjoyed most, I don’t volunteer for it now unless someone else is handling the planning. If I have to choose the book and think through the timing and make all the little decisions, I start second-guessing everything.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely interfering. It’s keeping me from getting back into the classroom in any reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your relationships with family and friends are being affected by how you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s mainly when I’m thinking about teaching, I think. That’s where it really takes over.

I mean, I can feel self-conscious in normal situations like anyone else, but the kind of spiraling I’m talking about seems tied to being seen as a teacher — whether I’m competent, whether parents trust me, whether other teachers or administrators think I’m still capable. My mind keeps circling from one thing to the next: will I ever get hired again, have I ruined my reputation with parents, is retirement slipping farther away because I can’t get back into a steady job, and then even these tiny moments start feeling enormous.

Like with story-time, it should just be choosing a book and reading to children. But if I imagine choosing the wrong book or losing their attention or making some small mistake, my mind jumps all the way to, “Maybe this means I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore.” And I know that sounds like a huge leap. I can hear how harsh it sounds when I say it. But in the moment it feels like one mistake could prove something terrible about me.

So… yes, it’s mostly teaching. Or anything connected to teaching, really. That’s where the fear of being judged gets so big that I can’t keep it in proportion.

I’ll pick it up and realize I’m still holding my neck and face so tight that it’s not relaxing at all. It’s just wearing me down, and I thought… okay, I probably need to talk to someone about this.\n\nDoctor: Is the tightness in your body constant throughout the day, or does it get worse at specific times or when you're doing certain activities?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty much there all day, honestly. I wouldn’t say it completely comes and goes. It’s more like my shoulders and neck are just set in this clenched position from the time I’m at work onward, like I’m bracing myself for something—like a classroom observation that never actually ends.\n\nIt probably becomes harder to ignore as the day goes on. By evening, my jaw is aching too, like I’ve been holding my face tight without realizing it. That’s when it really hits me, because I’ll try to sit down and play guitar, which usually helps me unwind, and I can’t really enjoy it. I’m still so tense in my neck and jaw that even doing something I like feels uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel pain in your shoulders, neck, or jaw when you're tense?\n\nPatient: Yes—definitely. It’s not just tightness like, “oh, I’m a little stiff.” My shoulders and neck actually hurt from being clenched so much. It starts aching pretty badly, like I’ve been clenching my teeth or holding my face tense without noticing. That’s honestly one of the things that made me think I needed to come in, because I’ll try to do something I usually enjoy, like playing guitar, and I can hardly relax into it. I’m sitting there with my neck and jaw hurting, and it kind of ruins the one thing that’s supposed to help me unwind.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sleep has been… pretty bad, honestly. Most nights I get into bed already exhausted, but then my mind just starts running. I’ll lie there for hours replaying things—like interview answers, or whether I said something wrong, or bills I need to pay, or licensing forms I haven’t dealt with yet. It’s like once the house is quiet, my brain decides that’s the perfect time to review every possible problem.\n\nAnd even when I do finally fall asleep, it doesn’t feel like I’m getting real rest. I’ll wake up around four in the morning, already tense, already sort of calculating what could go wrong that day. Before I’ve even gotten out of bed, I’m thinking through everything I need to handle and all the ways it could fall apart. So yes, sleep has definitely been a problem.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble concentrating or completing tasks at work or at home because of how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. That’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because I’m not just sitting there avoiding things—I’ll be trying to do them, and my brain just won’t stay on the page.\n\nLike if I’m filling out applications, or even trying to plan what should be a simple lesson for the kids, I’ll read the same paragraph over and over and realize I haven’t taken it in at all. My eyes are moving across the words, but my attention has already jumped somewhere else—like what an administrator might think of what I wrote, or whether I missed some crucial detail that’s going to come back and embarrass me later.\n\nAnd then I’ll go back to check it, but checking it doesn’t really reassure me. It just makes the whole thing take twice as long. Something that should be straightforward ends up feeling like this huge task because I keep losing the thread. At work especially, lesson planning used to feel pretty natural to me, and now I can get stuck on one small part because I’m second-guessing every word or every choice.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or activities with friends and family because of how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes… although it’s less like I’m avoiding family dinners or something, and more that I’ve pulled back from the school-related things that used to make me feel connected to people.\n\nI used to volunteer for story-time pretty readily, for example. I liked doing that. Reading to the kids, picking out the books, making it feel fun—that used to be one of the parts of teaching that felt most natural to me. But lately I won’t put myself forward for it unless someone else is handling the planning side. If I have to choose the story, think through the timing, worry about whether it’s appropriate or engaging enough, I just get so wound up that I’d rather not do it at all.\n\nAnd it’s also affected trying to get back into the classroom. I’ve missed application deadlines because I kept putting off finishing things or checking them over too many times. I’ve gone into interviews and just… stumbled through them. I’ll know what I want to say, but I get so caught up in whether I’m answering correctly that I lose my train of thought. So yes, I guess I am avoiding things, or at least stepping back from them, and it’s making it hard to get back into teaching in any reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to fulfill your responsibilities as a preschool teacher due to how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I’m not really functioning the way I need to be, professionally. I keep telling myself I’m still a teacher, that I still know how to do the work, but actually getting back into a classroom has become… much harder than it should be.\n\nI’ve missed application deadlines because I couldn’t get the materials finished or I kept going back over them again and again until suddenly the deadline had passed. And when I do get to an interview, I stumble through it. I’ll know what I want to say, but I get so caught up in whether I’m saying it the “right” way that I lose the thread.\n\nEven with something like story-time, which used to be simple and honestly one of the parts I enjoyed most, I don’t volunteer for it now unless someone else is handling the planning. If I have to choose the book and think through the timing and make all the little decisions, I start second-guessing everything.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely interfering. It’s keeping me from getting back into the classroom in any reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your relationships with family and friends are being affected by how you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Not in a way I can point to clearly, like, “this relationship has changed” or “we had a big conflict because of it.” I don’t think I’ve noticed anything that definite with family or friends.\n\nMost of what I’m aware of has been around work and school-related situations, so when you ask about relationships, I have to sort of stop and think. I suppose people close to me may notice I’m not quite as available or as easygoing, but I don’t know that I can say it’s really affected the relationships themselves. I haven’t been tracking it that way.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is, I’m not sure. Nothing obvious comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or hobbies, aside from playing guitar, that you've had to stop or significantly reduce due to how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Aside from guitar… the biggest thing is really the school-related stuff, the activities that used to feel like part of who I was as a teacher.\n\nStory-time is the clearest example. I used to volunteer for that without really thinking twice. I liked choosing the books and figuring out how to make it engaging for the kids. Trying to get back into the classroom at all has been affected. I’ve missed application deadlines because I keep putting off finishing things or checking them over and over, and then the deadline is gone. I’ve had interviews where I just stumble through, even though I know I’m qualified and I know what I mean to say. So yes, I’ve reduced things quite a bit. It feels like I’m stepping back from the parts of teaching that used to connect me to the work, and it’s keeping me from getting back into a classroom in any steady way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your overall mood or emotional state since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that clearly. That’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because I’m not just sitting there avoiding things—I’ll be trying to do them, and my brain just won’t stay on the page.\n\nLike if I’m filling out applications, or even trying to plan what should be a simple lesson for the kids, I’ll read the same paragraph over and over and realize I haven’t taken it in at all. My eyes are moving across the words, but my attention has already jumped somewhere else—like what an administrator might think of what I wrote, or whether I missed some crucial detail that’s going to come back and embarrass me later.\n\nAnd then I’ll go back to check it, but checking it doesn’t really reassure me. It just makes the whole thing take twice as long. Something that should be straightforward ends up feeling like this huge task because I keep losing the thread. At work especially, lesson planning used to feel pretty natural to me, and now I can get stuck on one small part because I’m second-guessing every word or every choice.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or activities with friends and family because of how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes… although it’s less like I’m avoiding family dinners or something, and more that I’ve pulled back from the school-related things that used to make me feel connected to people.\n\nI used to volunteer for story-time pretty readily, for example. I liked doing that. Reading to the kids, picking out the books, making it feel fun—that used to be one of the parts of teaching that felt most natural to me. But lately I won’t put myself forward for it unless someone else is handling the planning side. If I have to choose the story, think through the timing, worry about whether it’s appropriate or engaging enough, I just get so wound up that I’d rather not do it at all.\n\nAnd it’s also affected trying to get back into the classroom. I’ve missed application deadlines because I kept putting off finishing things or checking them over too many times. I’ve gone into interviews and just… stumbled through them. I’ll know what I want to say, but I get so caught up in whether I’m answering correctly that I lose my train of thought. So yes, I guess I am avoiding things, or at least stepping back from them, and it’s making it hard to get back into teaching in any reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to fulfill your responsibilities as a preschool teacher due to how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I’m not really functioning the way I need to be, professionally. I keep telling myself I’m still a teacher, that I still know how to do the work, but actually getting back into a classroom has become… much harder than it should be.\n\nI’ve missed application deadlines because I couldn’t get the materials finished or I kept going back over them again and again until suddenly the deadline had passed. And when I do get to an interview, I stumble through it. I’ll know what I want to say, but I get so caught up in whether I’m saying it the “right” way that I lose the thread.\n\nEven with something like story-time, which used to be simple and honestly one of the parts I enjoyed most, I don’t volunteer for it now unless someone else is handling the planning. If I have to choose the book and think through the timing and make all the little decisions, I start second-guessing everything.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely interfering. It’s keeping me from getting back into the classroom in any reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your relationships with family and friends are being affected by how you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Not in a way I can point to clearly, like, “this relationship has changed” or “we had a big conflict because of it.” I don’t think I’ve noticed anything that definite with family or friends.\n\nMost of what I’m aware of has been around work and school-related situations, so when you ask about relationships, I have to sort of stop and think. I suppose people close to me may notice I’m not quite as available or as easygoing, but I don’t know that I can say it’s really affected the relationships themselves. I haven’t been tracking it that way.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is, I’m not sure. Nothing obvious comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or hobbies, aside from playing guitar, that you've had to stop or significantly reduce due to how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Aside from guitar… the biggest thing is really the school-related stuff, the activities that used to feel like part of who I was as a teacher.\n\nStory-time is the clearest example. I used to volunteer for that without really thinking twice. I liked choosing the books and figuring out how to make it engaging for the kids. But now I’ve pretty much stopped offering unless someone else is doing the planning part. If someone says, “We already picked the book, we just need you to read,” I can usually manage that. But if I have to decide what to read, how long it should take, whether it’s the right choice, whether I’ll keep their attention… I get so tangled up in it that I just don’t put myself forward.\n\nAnd it’s not only that one activity. Trying to get back into the classroom at all has been affected. I’ve missed application deadlines because I keep putting off finishing things or checking them over and over, and then the deadline is gone. I’ve had interviews where I just stumble through, even though I know I’m qualified and I know what I mean to say. So yes, I’ve reduced things quite a bit. It feels like I’m stepping back from the parts of teaching that used to connect me to the work, and it’s keeping me from getting back into a classroom in any steady way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your overall mood or emotional state since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that clearly. I mean, I know I don’t feel like myself in some ways, but if you’re asking whether I’ve had a major mood change, like feeling suddenly very low or unusually up or something like that, I don’t know that I can point to anything specific.\n\nIt’s more that my emotional state feels… crowded, if that makes sense. But I haven’t really been tracking it as a mood change. I’ve been so focused on whether I can get things done and whether I’m managing situations properly that I haven’t stepped back and thought, “My overall mood is different now.”\n\nSo I guess I’d say I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed one clear, significant change that I can describe neatly.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of being judged or evaluated by others in your teaching role?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I am. Maybe more than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s not just “Will they like my lesson?” or “Will the kids stay engaged?” It turns into this whole chain in my head. I start wondering whether anyone will hire me again, whether I’ve somehow damaged my reputation with parents or other teachers, whether people already see me as unreliable or not really fit for the classroom anymore. And then it keeps going — like, if I make a small mistake during story-time, choose the wrong book, lose the children’s attention for a minute, stumble over something… my mind jumps to, “Does that mean I shouldn’t be trusted with children?” Which sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but that’s where my thinking goes.\n\nAnd then there’s the bigger picture too. I’ll start worrying about whether I’ve messed up my whole future, whether retirement is getting farther away because I’m not getting back into a steady position, whether I’ve let too much time pass. So yes, I do feel afraid of being evaluated. It’s like every little teaching-related moment feels like it could confirm the worst thing I’m already afraid people might think about me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of being judged or evaluated something that you experience in other areas of your life, or is it mainly when you're thinking about teaching?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly when I’m thinking about teaching, I think. That’s where it really takes over.\n\nI mean, I can feel self-conscious in normal situations like anyone else, but the kind of spiraling I’m talking about seems tied to being seen as a teacher — whether I’m competent, whether parents trust me, whether other teachers or administrators think I’m still capable. My mind keeps circling from one thing to the next: will I ever get hired again, have I ruined my reputation with parents, is retirement slipping farther away because I can’t get back into a steady job, and then even these tiny moments start feeling enormous.\n\nLike with story-time, it should just be choosing a book and reading to children. But if I imagine choosing the wrong book or losing their attention or making some small mistake, my mind jumps all the way to, “Maybe this means I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore.” And I know that sounds like a huge leap. I can hear how harsh it sounds when I say it. But in the moment it feels like one mistake could prove something terrible about me.\n\nSo… yes, it’s mostly teaching. Or anything connected to teaching, really. That’s where the fear of being judged gets so big that I can’t keep it in proportion.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. It causes me a lot of distress. More than I think I’ve really been admitting to myself.

It’s not just that I worry someone might think I did a poor job. It’s that my mind doesn’t stop there. It keeps circling and expanding, almost like one concern hooks onto another. I’ll start with, “Will I ever get hired again?” and then it becomes, “Have I ruined my reputation with parents?” and then, “Do other people in the field already see me as someone who isn’t dependable anymore?” And then it gets even bigger, like, “Is retirement getting farther away because I can’t get back into steady work?” It becomes this whole chain, and I can feel myself getting more and more trapped in it.

And the thing that bothers me is how small the trigger can be. Something like story-time — which should be familiar to me, something I know how to do — can turn into this huge source of fear. If I imagine choosing the wrong book, or losing the children’s attention for a moment, or making some little mistake, my mind jumps to, “Maybe this means I shouldn’t be trusted with children.” And I know that sounds awful and excessive when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real.

So yes, it’s distressing. It’s not just a passing worry. It feels like my brain keeps putting me on trial over and over again.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, what finally pushed me to come in is this constant tightness in my body. My shoulders and neck are just… clenched all day. I wouldn’t say it completely comes and goes. It starts aching pretty badly, like I’ve been clenching my teeth or holding my face tense without noticing. I’m sitting there with my neck and jaw hurting, and it kind of ruins the one thing that’s supposed to help me unwind.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sleep has been… pretty bad, honestly. Most nights I get into bed already exhausted, but then my mind just starts running. I’ll lie there for hours replaying things—like interview answers, or whether I said something wrong, or bills I need to pay, or licensing forms I haven’t dealt with yet. It’s like once the house is quiet, my brain decides that’s the perfect time to review every possible problem.\n\nAnd even when I do finally fall asleep, it doesn’t feel like I’m getting real rest. I’ll wake up around four in the morning, already tense, already sort of calculating what could go wrong that day. Before I’ve even gotten out of bed, I’m thinking through everything I need to handle and all the ways it could fall apart. So yes, sleep has definitely been a problem.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble concentrating or completing tasks at work or at home because of how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. That’s actually one of the more frustrating parts, because I’m not just sitting there avoiding things—I’ll be trying to do them, and my brain just won’t stay on the page.\n\nLike if I’m filling out applications, or even trying to plan what should be a simple lesson for the kids, I’ll read the same paragraph over and over and realize I haven’t taken it in at all. My eyes are moving across the words, but my attention has already jumped somewhere else—like what an administrator might think of what I wrote, or whether I missed some crucial detail that’s going to come back and embarrass me later.\n\nAnd then I’ll go back to check it, but checking it doesn’t really reassure me. It just makes the whole thing take twice as long. Something that should be straightforward ends up feeling like this huge task because I keep losing the thread. At work especially, lesson planning used to feel pretty natural to me, and now I can get stuck on one small part because I’m second-guessing every word or every choice.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or activities with friends and family because of how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes… although it’s less like I’m avoiding family dinners or something, and more that I’ve pulled back from the school-related things that used to make me feel connected to people.\n\nI used to volunteer for story-time pretty readily, for example. I liked doing that. Reading to the kids, picking out the books, making it feel fun—that used to be one of the parts of teaching that felt most natural to me. But lately I won’t put myself forward for it unless someone else is handling the planning side. If I have to choose the story, think through the timing, worry about whether it’s appropriate or engaging enough, I just get so wound up that I’d rather not do it at all.\n\nAnd it’s also affected trying to get back into the classroom. I’ve missed application deadlines because I kept putting off finishing things or checking them over too many times. I’ve gone into interviews and just… stumbled through them. I’ll know what I want to say, but I get so caught up in whether I’m answering correctly that I lose my train of thought. So yes, I guess I am avoiding things, or at least stepping back from them, and it’s making it hard to get back into teaching in any reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to fulfill your responsibilities as a preschool teacher due to how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I’m not really functioning the way I need to be, professionally. I keep telling myself I’m still a teacher, that I still know how to do the work, but actually getting back into a classroom has become… much harder than it should be.\n\nI’ve missed application deadlines because I couldn’t get the materials finished or I kept going back over them again and again until suddenly the deadline had passed. And when I do get to an interview, I stumble through it. I’ll know what I want to say, but I get so caught up in whether I’m saying it the “right” way that I lose the thread.\n\nEven with something like story-time, which used to be simple and honestly one of the parts I enjoyed most, I don’t volunteer for it now unless someone else is handling the planning. If I have to choose the book and think through the timing and make all the little decisions, I start second-guessing everything.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely interfering. It’s keeping me from getting back into the classroom in any reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your relationships with family and friends are being affected by how you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Not in a way I can point to clearly, like, “this relationship has changed” or “we had a big conflict because of it.” I don’t think I’ve noticed anything that definite with family or friends.\n\nMost of what I’m aware of has been around work and school-related situations, so when you ask about relationships, I have to sort of stop and think. I suppose people close to me may notice I’m not quite as available or as easygoing, but I don’t know that I can say it’s really affected the relationships themselves. I haven’t been tracking it that way.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is, I’m not sure. Nothing obvious comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or hobbies, aside from playing guitar, that you've had to stop or significantly reduce due to how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Aside from guitar… the biggest thing is really the school-related stuff, the activities that used to feel like part of who I was as a teacher.\n\nStory-time is the clearest example. I used to volunteer for that without really thinking twice. I liked choosing the books and figuring out how to make it engaging for the kids. But now I’ve pretty much stopped offering unless someone else is doing the planning part. If someone says, “We already picked the book, we just need you to read,” I can usually manage that. But if I have to decide what to read, how long it should take, whether it’s the right choice, whether I’ll keep their attention… I get so tangled up in it that I just don’t put myself forward.\n\nAnd it’s not only that one activity. Trying to get back into the classroom at all has been affected. I’ve missed application deadlines because I keep putting off finishing things or checking them over and over, and then the deadline is gone. I’ve had interviews where I just stumble through, even though I know I’m qualified and I know what I mean to say. So yes, I’ve reduced things quite a bit. It feels like I’m stepping back from the parts of teaching that used to connect me to the work, and it’s keeping me from getting back into a classroom in any steady way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your overall mood or emotional state since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that clearly. I mean, I know I don’t feel like myself in some ways, but if you’re asking whether I’ve had a major mood change, like feeling suddenly very low or unusually up or something like that, I don’t know that I can point to anything specific.\n\nIt’s more that my emotional state feels… crowded, if that makes sense. But I haven’t really been tracking it as a mood change. I’ve been so focused on whether I can get things done and whether I’m managing situations properly that I haven’t stepped back and thought, “My overall mood is different now.”\n\nSo I guess I’d say I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed one clear, significant change that I can describe neatly.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of being judged or evaluated by others in your teaching role?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I am. Maybe more than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s not just “Will they like my lesson?” or “Will the kids stay engaged?” It turns into this whole chain in my head. I start wondering whether anyone will hire me again, whether I’ve somehow damaged my reputation with parents or other teachers, whether people already see me as unreliable or not really fit for the classroom anymore. And then it keeps going — like, if I make a small mistake during story-time, choose the wrong book, lose the children’s attention for a minute, stumble over something… my mind jumps to, “Does that mean I shouldn’t be trusted with children?” Which sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but that’s where my thinking goes.\n\nAnd then there’s the bigger picture too. I’ll start worrying about whether I’ve messed up my whole future, whether retirement is getting farther away because I’m not getting back into a steady position, whether I’ve let too much time pass. So yes, I do feel afraid of being evaluated. It’s like every little teaching-related moment feels like it could confirm the worst thing I’m already afraid people might think about me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of being judged or evaluated something that you experience in other areas of your life, or is it mainly when you're thinking about teaching?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly when I’m thinking about teaching, I think. That’s where it really takes over.\n\nI mean, I can feel self-conscious in normal situations like anyone else, but the kind of spiraling I’m talking about seems tied to being seen as a teacher — whether I’m competent, whether parents trust me, whether other teachers or administrators think I’m still capable. My mind keeps circling from one thing to the next: will I ever get hired again, have I ruined my reputation with parents, is retirement slipping farther away because I can’t get back into a steady job, and then even these tiny moments start feeling enormous.\n\nLike with story-time, it should just be choosing a book and reading to children. But if I imagine choosing the wrong book or losing their attention or making some small mistake, my mind jumps all the way to, “Maybe this means I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore.” And I know that sounds like a huge leap. I can hear how harsh it sounds when I say it. But in the moment it feels like one mistake could prove something terrible about me.\n\nSo… yes, it’s mostly teaching. Or anything connected to teaching, really. That’s where the fear of being judged gets so big that I can’t keep it in proportion.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of being judged or evaluated is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. It causes me a lot of distress. More than I think I’ve really been admitting to myself.\n\nIt’s not just that I worry someone might think I did a poor job. It’s that my mind doesn’t stop there. It keeps circling and expanding, almost like one concern hooks onto another. I’ll start with, “Will I ever get hired again?” and then it becomes, “Have I ruined my reputation with parents?” and then, “Do other people in the field already see me as someone who isn’t dependable anymore?” And then it gets even bigger, like, “Is retirement getting farther away because I can’t get back into steady work?” It becomes this whole chain, and I can feel myself getting more and more trapped in it.\n\nAnd the thing that bothers me is how small the trigger can be. Something like story-time — which should be familiar to me, something I know how to do — can turn into this huge source of fear. If I imagine choosing the wrong book, or losing the children’s attention for a moment, or making some little mistake, my mind jumps to, “Maybe this means I shouldn’t be trusted with children.” And I know that sounds awful and excessive when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nSo yes, it’s distressing. It’s not just a passing worry. It feels like my brain keeps putting me on trial over and over again.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

And then it keeps going — like, if I make a small mistake during story-time, choose the wrong book, lose the children’s attention for a minute, stumble over something… my mind jumps to, “Does that mean I shouldn’t be trusted with children?” Which sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but that’s where my thinking goes.\n\nAnd then there’s the bigger picture too. I’ll start worrying about whether I’ve messed up my whole future, whether retirement is getting farther away because I’m not getting back into a steady position, whether I’ve let too much time pass. So yes, I do feel afraid of being evaluated. It’s like every little teaching-related moment feels like it could confirm the worst thing I’m already afraid people might think about me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of being judged or evaluated something that you experience in other areas of your life, or is it mainly when you're thinking about teaching?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly when I’m thinking about teaching, I think. That’s where it really takes over.\n\nI mean, I can feel self-conscious in normal situations like anyone else, but the kind of spiraling I’m talking about seems tied to being seen as a teacher — whether I’m competent, whether parents trust me, whether other teachers or administrators think I’m still capable. My mind keeps circling from one thing to the next: will I ever get hired again, have I ruined my reputation with parents, is retirement slipping farther away because I can’t get back into a steady job, and then even these tiny moments start feeling enormous.\n\nLike with story-time, it should just be choosing a book and reading to children. It keeps circling and expanding, almost like one concern hooks onto another. I’ll start with, “Will I ever get hired again?” and then it becomes, “Have I ruined my reputation with parents?” and then, “Do other people in the field already see me as someone who isn’t dependable anymore?” And then it gets even bigger, like, “Is retirement getting farther away because I can’t get back into steady work?” It becomes this whole chain, and I can feel myself getting more and more trapped in it.\n\nAnd the thing that bothers me is how small the trigger can be. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not completely sure how to separate it, honestly. I don’t think I walk around feeling judged in every part of my life in the same way. Like if I’m at the grocery store or talking to a neighbor, I might have the usual self-conscious moments, but it doesn’t become this big… trial in my head.

The teaching piece is where it feels really charged. That’s where being judged feels tied to whether I’m competent, whether I’m trusted, whether I still have a place in that world. Outside of that, I don’t notice it as much, or at least it doesn’t grab hold of me with the same intensity.

So I guess my answer is… not really, not in a broad way. Or if it does happen outside teaching, I haven’t been as aware of it. It’s mainly anything connected to being seen as a teacher that makes me feel exposed and evaluated.
