At work especially, lesson planning used to feel pretty natural to me, and now I can get stuck on one small part because I’m second-guessing every word or every choice.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or activities with friends and family because of how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes… although it’s less like I’m avoiding family dinners or something, and more that I’ve pulled back from the school-related things that used to make me feel connected to people.\n\nI used to volunteer for story-time pretty readily, for example. I liked doing that. Reading to the kids, picking out the books, making it feel fun—that used to be one of the parts of teaching that felt most natural to me. But lately I won’t put myself forward for it unless someone else is handling the planning side. If I have to choose the story, think through the timing, worry about whether it’s appropriate or engaging enough, I just get so wound up that I’d rather not do it at all.\n\nAnd it’s also affected trying to get back into the classroom. I’ve missed application deadlines because I kept putting off finishing things or checking them over too many times. I’ve gone into interviews and just… stumbled through them. I’ll know what I want to say, but I get so caught up in whether I’m answering correctly that I lose my train of thought. So yes, I guess I am avoiding things, or at least stepping back from them, and it’s making it hard to get back into teaching in any reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to fulfill your responsibilities as a preschool teacher due to how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I’m not really functioning the way I need to be, professionally. I keep telling myself I’m still a teacher, that I still know how to do the work, but actually getting back into a classroom has become… much harder than it should be.\n\nI’ve missed application deadlines because I couldn’t get the materials finished or I kept going back over them again and again until suddenly the deadline had passed. And when I do get to an interview, I stumble through it. I’ll know what I want to say, but I get so caught up in whether I’m saying it the “right” way that I lose the thread.\n\nEven with something like story-time, which used to be simple and honestly one of the parts I enjoyed most, I don’t volunteer for it now unless someone else is handling the planning. If I have to choose the book and think through the timing and make all the little decisions, I start second-guessing everything.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely interfering. It’s keeping me from getting back into the classroom in any reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your relationships with family and friends are being affected by how you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Not in a way I can point to clearly, like, “this relationship has changed” or “we had a big conflict because of it.” I don’t think I’ve noticed anything that definite with family or friends.\n\nMost of what I’m aware of has been around work and school-related situations, so when you ask about relationships, I have to sort of stop and think. I suppose people close to me may notice I’m not quite as available or as easygoing, but I don’t know that I can say it’s really affected the relationships themselves. I haven’t been tracking it that way.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is, I’m not sure. Nothing obvious comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or hobbies, aside from playing guitar, that you've had to stop or significantly reduce due to how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Aside from guitar… the biggest thing is really the school-related stuff, the activities that used to feel like part of who I was as a teacher.\n\nStory-time is the clearest example. I used to volunteer for that without really thinking twice. I liked choosing the books and figuring out how to make it engaging for the kids. But now I’ve pretty much stopped offering unless someone else is doing the planning part. If someone says, “We already picked the book, we just need you to read,” I can usually manage that. But if I have to decide what to read, how long it should take, whether it’s the right choice, whether I’ll keep their attention… I get so tangled up in it that I just don’t put myself forward.\n\nAnd it’s not only that one activity. Trying to get back into the classroom at all has been affected. I’ve missed application deadlines because I keep putting off finishing things or checking them over and over, and then the deadline is gone. I’ve had interviews where I just stumble through, even though I know I’m qualified and I know what I mean to say. So yes, I’ve reduced things quite a bit. It feels like I’m stepping back from the parts of teaching that used to connect me to the work, and it’s keeping me from getting back into a classroom in any steady way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your overall mood or emotional state since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that clearly. I mean, I know I don’t feel like myself in some ways, but if you’re asking whether I’ve had a major mood change, like feeling suddenly very low or unusually up or something like that, I don’t know that I can point to anything specific.\n\nIt’s more that my emotional state feels… crowded, if that makes sense. But I haven’t really been tracking it as a mood change. I’ve been so focused on whether I can get things done and whether I’m managing situations properly that I haven’t stepped back and thought, “My overall mood is different now.”\n\nSo I guess I’d say I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed one clear, significant change that I can describe neatly.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of being judged or evaluated by others in your teaching role?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I am. Maybe more than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s not just “Will they like my lesson?” or “Will the kids stay engaged?” It turns into this whole chain in my head. I start wondering whether anyone will hire me again, whether I’ve somehow damaged my reputation with parents or other teachers, whether people already see me as unreliable or not really fit for the classroom anymore. And then it keeps going — like, if I make a small mistake during story-time, choose the wrong book, lose the children’s attention for a minute, stumble over something… my mind jumps to, “Does that mean I shouldn’t be trusted with children?” Which sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but that’s where my thinking goes.\n\nAnd then there’s the bigger picture too. I’ll start worrying about whether I’ve messed up my whole future, whether retirement is getting farther away because I’m not getting back into a steady position, whether I’ve let too much time pass. So yes, I do feel afraid of being evaluated. It’s like every little teaching-related moment feels like it could confirm the worst thing I’m already afraid people might think about me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of being judged or evaluated something that you experience in other areas of your life, or is it mainly when you're thinking about teaching?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly when I’m thinking about teaching, I think. That’s where it really takes over.\n\nI mean, I can feel self-conscious in normal situations like anyone else, but the kind of spiraling I’m talking about seems tied to being seen as a teacher — whether I’m competent, whether parents trust me, whether other teachers or administrators think I’m still capable. My mind keeps circling from one thing to the next: will I ever get hired again, have I ruined my reputation with parents, is retirement slipping farther away because I can’t get back into a steady job, and then even these tiny moments start feeling enormous.\n\nLike with story-time, it should just be choosing a book and reading to children. But if I imagine choosing the wrong book or losing their attention or making some small mistake, my mind jumps all the way to, “Maybe this means I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore.” And I know that sounds like a huge leap. I can hear how harsh it sounds when I say it. But in the moment it feels like one mistake could prove something terrible about me.\n\nSo… yes, it’s mostly teaching. Or anything connected to teaching, really. That’s where the fear of being judged gets so big that I can’t keep it in proportion.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of being judged or evaluated is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. It causes me a lot of distress. More than I think I’ve really been admitting to myself.\n\nIt’s not just that I worry someone might think I did a poor job. It’s that my mind doesn’t stop there. It keeps circling and expanding, almost like one concern hooks onto another. I’ll start with, “Will I ever get hired again?” and then it becomes, “Have I ruined my reputation with parents?” and then, “Do other people in the field already see me as someone who isn’t dependable anymore?” And then it gets even bigger, like, “Is retirement getting farther away because I can’t get back into steady work?” It becomes this whole chain, and I can feel myself getting more and more trapped in it.\n\nAnd the thing that bothers me is how small the trigger can be. Something like story-time — which should be familiar to me, something I know how to do — can turn into this huge source of fear. If I imagine choosing the wrong book, or losing the children’s attention for a moment, or making some little mistake, my mind jumps to, “Maybe this means I shouldn’t be trusted with children.” And I know that sounds awful and excessive when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nSo yes, it’s distressing. It’s not just a passing worry. It feels like my brain keeps putting me on trial over and over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this fear of being judged in areas of your life outside of teaching?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate it, honestly. I don’t think I walk around feeling judged in every part of my life in the same way. Like if I’m at the grocery store or talking to a neighbor, I might have the usual self-conscious moments, but it doesn’t become this big… trial in my head.\n\nThe teaching piece is where it feels really charged. That’s where being judged feels tied to whether I’m competent, whether I’m trusted, whether I still have a place in that world. Outside of that, I don’t notice it as much, or at least it doesn’t grab hold of me with the same intensity.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… not really, not in a broad way. Or if it does happen outside teaching, I haven’t been as aware of it. It’s mainly anything connected to being seen as a teacher that makes me feel exposed and evaluated.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel this fear of being judged if you were to switch to a completely different career?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I’ve thought about that, but I don’t feel like I have a clean answer.\n\nPart of me thinks if I left teaching completely, maybe that particular fear would quiet down, because so much of it is wrapped up in being seen as responsible for children and being trusted in that role. That’s where I feel like every little mistake means something bigger about who I am.\n\nBut then another part of me wonders if I’d just transfer the same fear onto whatever the new job was. Like if I were working somewhere totally different, would I start worrying that people thought I was incompetent there too? Would I still feel watched or measured? I don’t really know, because I haven’t been in that situation.\n\nI guess what I can say is that I don’t notice it as strongly outside of teaching right now. The fear feels very tied to teaching and my identity as a teacher. But whether changing careers would make it go away completely… I’m not sure. I’d like to think it would help, but I can’t say that with confidence.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of being judged would decrease significantly if you were no longer in a teaching position?\n\nPatient: I think it would probably decrease some, yes, because teaching is where it feels most intense. If I weren’t in that position anymore, I wouldn’t be facing those same situations that set it off — parents watching, administrators judging, children depending on me, even something like story-time feeling like some kind of test.\n\nBut I don’t know if it would disappear. That’s the part I get stuck on. My mind doesn’t just have one worry and stop. It keeps circling. I’ll start with, “Will I ever get hired again?” and then it turns into, “Have I ruined my reputation with parents?” and then, “Do people already see me as unreliable?” And then it goes even further, like, “Is retirement slipping farther away because I can’t get back into steady work?”\n\nSo if I were no longer teaching, maybe the immediate fear of being judged in the classroom would lessen. But I’m not sure the whole pattern would go away, because even a small thing — like imagining I picked the wrong book for story-time or lost the children’s attention — can turn into, “Maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children at all.” It gets so much bigger than the actual moment.\n\nSo… yes, I think being out of teaching might reduce it significantly in that specific area. But I’m afraid my mind would still find a way to keep questioning whether I’ve failed or damaged things somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would worry about being judged or evaluated if you were doing something you considered a hobby or something you were not being paid for?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I think it would depend a lot on what the hobby was and who was around.\n\nIf it was something completely private, like reading or doing something at home where no one else was seeing it or grading it, then probably not in the same way. I don’t think I’d sit there worrying about being judged if there wasn’t really anyone involved.\n\nBut if it was a hobby where people could see me doing it — like a class, or a group activity, or something where someone might comment on how well I was doing — then maybe I would. I don’t know that it would have the same weight as teaching, because teaching feels tied to responsibility and competence and being trusted. A hobby feels lower stakes, at least in theory.\n\nBut I can imagine myself still getting self-conscious. Like if I were doing something badly, or if I thought people were noticing, I might start thinking, “Do they think I’m ridiculous?” or “Do I look like I don’t know what I’m doing?” It probably wouldn’t hit the same deep nerve as being judged as a teacher, but I can’t say I’d be completely free of it either.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is, I don’t really know. I think if it wasn’t paid and wasn’t connected to children or my work, the fear would likely be less. But if there were other people watching or evaluating me, I might still worry some.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would experience this same level of fear of being judged if you were in a different profession or role that didn't involve teaching?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it would be the exact same level, because teaching is such a specific trigger for me. It’s not just a job in my mind — it’s tied to being trusted with children, and that makes every mistake feel like it says something serious about me.\n\nBut I do worry that the pattern would follow me in some form. My mind doesn’t just stop at one concern. It keeps circling and expanding. Like, it starts with, “Will I ever get hired again?” and then it turns into, “Have I ruined my reputation with parents?” and then, “Do people already think I’m unreliable?” And then it somehow reaches things like, “Is retirement slipping farther away because I can’t get back into steady work?”\n\nSo if I were in a different profession, maybe I wouldn’t have the exact same fear about parents or children or being judged as a teacher. The teaching part makes it worse, but the circling worry feels like it could attach itself to something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would experience fear of being judged in a situation where you are not being evaluated or graded, but still performing a task in front of others?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I probably would, though maybe the intensity would depend on the situation.\n\nEven if no one was officially grading me or evaluating me, if I was doing something in front of people, my mind would still start going. It’s like I can know, logically, “No one is judging this, this isn’t a formal test,” but that doesn’t stop the thoughts from circling. I’d start wondering whether I looked competent, whether people were noticing little mistakes, whether they were thinking I didn’t know what I was doing.\n\nAnd then it doesn’t always stay small. That’s the part that bothers me. My mind can jump from one thing to the next so quickly. It starts with, “What if I didn’t do that right?” and then suddenly I’m thinking, “Will I ever get hired again?” or “Have I ruined my reputation with parents?” or “Do people already see me as unreliable?” And then it can stretch even further, like, “Is retirement slipping away because I can’t get back into steady work?”\n\nEven with something as small as a story-time activity — like if I chose the wrong book or the children didn’t seem engaged — I can start turning that into something much bigger, like maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children at all. So if I were performing some task in front of others, even without being graded, I think that same kind of worry could still happen. Maybe not always as strongly, but yes, I think being watched would be enough to set it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of being judged would still be present if you were to completely retire from teaching and no longer be involved in the education field?\n\nPatient: I think it would still be there, yes… maybe in a different shape, but I don’t think it would just disappear because I stopped teaching.\n\nPart of me wants to say, “If I retired completely, then maybe I wouldn’t have to worry about parents judging me, or administrators, or whether I’m good enough in the classroom.” And maybe that particular pressure would ease. But the way my mind works, it doesn’t stay neatly in one box. It keeps circling.\n\nEven now, it goes from, “Will I ever get hired again?” to “Have I ruined my reputation with parents?” to “Do people think I’m unreliable?” And then it stretches into bigger life questions, like whether retirement is slipping farther away because I can’t get back into steady work. Even a small thing, like choosing the wrong book for story-time or not keeping the children engaged, can turn into this much larger fear that maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children at all. So if I were no longer in education, maybe that exact thought wouldn’t come up as often. But I’m not confident the fear of being judged would be gone. I think it would probably attach itself to the fact that I had left, or what people thought that meant about me."
